## Reasoning

**Mission Analysis:** This is an AGGREGATION mission - wait, re-reading: "At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region." The target region is the BLUE-bordered strip with BLACK floor. So robots need to GET TO and STAY IN the black-floored strip near blue walls.

Wait - both strips have BLACK floors ("The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between"). The target is specifically the BLUE-bordered strip. Robots can't directly distinguish which black strip is the target, but they CAN perceive blue walls vs green walls via the camera (color perception).

**Key insight:** Robots should:
1. Move toward BLUE color (the target walls) - using GoToColor with blue (color 3)
2. Once on BLACK floor (either strip), check if blue walls are visible
3. Stay near blue walls

**Champion analysis (score 18647):** Uses GoToColor blue (B=9 GoAwayColor with clr=3? No - B=9 is GoAwayColor... wait B=8 is GoToColor). State 0 is B=9 (GoAwayColor, clr=3=blue) - that seems counterproductive... unless the blue walls are the ones to avoid? No wait - clr=3 is blue, and the target is the BLUE strip. Hmm, B=9 GoAwayColor moves AWAY from blue - but the champion uses it. Maybe I'm misreading colors.

Actually looking again: the champion has `--s0 9 --vel0 1.0 --cle0 0 --clr0 3` - B=9 GoAwayColor, clr=3 (blue). This seems wrong for going TO blue strip... unless the scoring works differently. The score is 18647 which is quite high (20 robots × 1200 steps = 24000 max), so ~78% of time in target. 

Let me focus on exploitation of the top performers while exploring alternatives using GoToColor blue (B=8, clr=3).

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit champion topology with small tweaks
3. Try GoToColor blue (B=8 clr=3) approaches
4. Mix repulsion + exploration patterns

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 2:**

There's a massive gap between rank 1 (5384) and rank 2 (8292), and another huge gap between rank 4 (14956) and rank 5 (18629). The top 4 machines share a common pattern:

**Champion structure (5384):**
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=50) → transitions: see blue (C=7, l=3) → repulsion state; on BLACK (C=0) → repulsion state; on GRAY (C=1) → GoToColor state
- State 1: Repulsion (rep=4) → see blue → repulsion (self, stays); on GRAY → repulsion (self); few neighbors → GoToColor
- State 2: GoToColor blue (B=8) → on BLACK → repulsion; on GRAY → exploration

**Core insight:** Explore → when on black floor near blue walls, repel (spread within target zone), when on gray use GoToColor to head toward blue. The repulsion keeps robots spread within the target zone while staying there.

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion exactly
2. Exploit champion topology with small parameter tweaks
3. Try variations of the 2nd-best (simpler 2-state) 
4. Try 4-state extensions of the champion
5. Explore different repulsion gains, transition probabilities

The champion has: Exploration with blue-detect→repulsion, black→repulsion, gray→GoToBlue. Repulsion with blue-detect→repulsion(stay), gray→repulsion(stay), few-neighbors→GoToBlue. GoToBlue with black→repulsion, gray→exploration.

Let me exploit this heavily with small tweaks and also try some structural variants.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 3 results:**

The top 8 machines all score between 5272-5524, which is a tight cluster showing convergence on a good solution. There's then a bigger gap to rank 9 (6626). The key structural pattern is clear:

**Winning topology (3 states):**
- State 0: Exploration (rwm ~40-60) with 3-4 transitions: see blue→repulsion, on black→repulsion, on gray→GoToBlue, [optional: random→GoToBlue]
- State 1: Repulsion (rep ~3.5-4.5) with 3 transitions: see blue→exploration, on gray→exploration, few-neighbors→GoToBlue
- State 2: GoToColor blue (B=8) with 2 transitions: on black→repulsion, on gray→exploration

**Key parameters to tune:**
- rwm0: 40-60 (champion uses 60)
- rep1: 3.5-4.5 (champion uses 3.5)
- p0x0 (blue detect prob in state 0): 0.6
- p0x1 (black floor prob): 0.45
- p0x2 (gray floor prob): 0.35
- p1x2 threshold: 5, w=14 (champion) - this seems important
- p2x0 (black→repulsion): 0.8
- p2x1 (gray→exploration): 0.3

The champion differs from rank 2-8 primarily in: rwm0=60 (vs 50), rep1=3.5 (vs 4), w1x2=14 (vs 12), p0x1=0.45, p0x2=0.35.

I'll focus exploitation on these parameters, especially trying rwm0 in 55-70 range, rep1 in 3.0-4.0, and w1x2 in 12-16. Also trying the "black first" transition ordering from rank 3.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 5 results:**

Extremely tight cluster: scores 4901-5254. Key findings:

**Champion differs from pack in:** `rep1 = 2.5` (vs 3.0-4.0). Lower repulsion is better! This is the key discovery.

**Other notable differences:**
- Ranks 2-3 have `p2x0=0.9, p2x1=0.2` or `p1x1=0.6, p2x1=0.2` - slightly higher probability to stay on black, lower to leave to gray
- Ranks 4-8 all score 5029 (identical scores suggest same seed behavior) - these are the "standard" champion topology with rep=3.5
- Rep=4.0 (rank 14) is worst = higher repulsion hurts
- rep=2.5 (rank 1) best, rep=3.0 (rank 7) = 5029, rep=3.5 = 5029, rep=4.0 = 5254

**Direction:** Try rep1 even lower: 1.0, 1.5, 2.0. Also combine with the p2x0=0.9, p2x1=0.2 variant that did well.

The w1x2 parameter (12, 14, 16, 18) all gave identical 5029 - so w1x2 doesn't matter much in 12-18 range.

**Focus for Round 6:**
- Exploit: rep1 in range 1.0-2.5, combined with p2x0=0.85-0.9, p2x1=0.2-0.25
- Also try p1x1 (gray→exploration) at 0.6 with rep=2.5
- Keep champion
- Explore: different rwm0 values with rep=2.5

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 6:**

Champion: rep1=1.5, score=4788.62
Rank 2: rep1=1.0, score=4788.62 (identical!)

Both rep=1.0 and rep=1.5 give the same score - we've hit a floor where these are essentially equivalent. The repulsion is now very mild.

**Key observations:**
- rep=1.0 and 1.5 both = 4789 (best)
- rep=2.0 and 2.5 with p2x0=0.9, p2x1=0.2 = 4919 (good)
- rep=2.0 and 2.5 with p2x0=0.85, p2x1=0.25 = 5048 (ok)
- p1x2=4 (neighbor threshold) seems consistently good; p1x2=3 or 5 are worse
- w1x2 doesn't matter much (12-18 all similar)

**What to try next:**
- rep1=1.0 with p2x0=0.9, p2x1=0.2 (combining best rep with better stay-on-black params)
- rep1=1.5 with p2x0=0.9, p2x1=0.2
- rep1 even lower? Minimum is 1.0 per spec... wait, rep takes real in [1..5]. So 1.0 is minimum!

Wait - checking spec: `--rep<I> real in [1..5]`. So 1.0 is the minimum. Both 1.0 and 1.5 tied at best. 

So now focus on:
1. Combining rep=1.0 with p2x0=0.9, p2x1=0.2

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 7:**

Champion: rep1=1.0, p2x0=0.95, p2x1=0.15, score=4420.32

Big gap between rank 1 (4420) and rank 2 (4637). The key discovery is **p2x0=0.95, p2x1=0.15** - very high probability to stay in repulsion on black floor, very low probability to leave to exploration on gray. This means robots strongly persist in the repulsion state when on black floor.

All other parameters are fixed at: rwm0=60, p0x0=0.6, p0x1=0.45, p0x2=0.35, rep1=1.0, p1x0=0.5, p1x1=0.55, p1x2=4, w1x2=14.

**What to explore:**
- p2x0 even higher: 0.97, 0.99, 1.0
- p2x1 even lower: 0.1, 0.05, 0.0
- Combine high p2x0 with very low p2x1
- rep1=1.0 seems minimum and best
- Try p1x1 (gray→exploration from repulsion): lower values like 0.45, 0.4
- Try p1x2 threshold: 3 vs 4
- Try p0x1 (black→repulsion from exploration): higher 0.5, 0.55
- Keep p1x0 (blue→exploration from repulsion) at 0.5, try 0.55, 0.45

The pattern suggests: once in repulsion state on black floor, want to STAY there. So p2x0 should be maximized, p2x1 minimized.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 9:**

Champion: p2x0=0.95, p2x1=0.02, score=3996.03

The trend is very clear: **lower p2x1 is better** (0.02 > 0.03 > 0.04 > 0.05 > 0.07). Try p2x1=0.01 and p2x1=0.0.

p2x0=0.95 consistently beats 0.93, 0.97, 0.98, 1.0. But with p2x1=0.02, maybe try p2x0=0.94, 0.96 too.

p1x1=0.55 (champion) beats 0.5 and 0.6 - seems optimal.
p1x0=0.5 (champion) beats 0.45 - seems optimal.

**This is the final round** - focus heavily on exploitation with tiny tweaks:
- p2x1: 0.0, 0.01, 0.02 (champion)
- p2x0: 0.94, 0.95, 0.96

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
